     1  # **Technical Document: Sequence Signature Encoding**
     2  
     3  This document describes, in detail, how the Sequence signature encoding 
is structured, how each part is packed into bytes, and how chained signatures 
and top-level signatures work. It is purely technical, explaining the bit 
layouts, flags, and usage with examples.
     4  
     5  ---
     6  
     7  ## **1. Overview**
     8  
     9  Sequence uses a specialized signature format that:
    10  
    11  1. Has a **top-level signature** that includes:
    12  
    13     - A single "signature flag" byte that encodes multiple fields 
(checkpointer usage, signature type, checkpoint size, threshold size, etc.).
    14     - (Optionally) data related to an on-chain checkpointer contract.
    15     - A notion of _chained signatures_ vs. _normal signatures_ vs. _‚Äúno
chain id‚Äù_ signatures.
    16     - A final threshold and checkpoint value that tie into the overall 
wallet or contract logic.
    17  
    18  2. Contains a **merkle-like structure** for the signers at the 
‚Äúbranch‚Äù level, where each ‚Äúleaf‚Äù or ‚Äúnode‚Äù is encoded using a 
separate mini-flag nibble. This is parsed with a loop in the `recoverBranch` 
function.
    19  
    20  3. Supports multiple sub-signature types, such as ECDSA 
(`FLAG_SIGNATURE_HASH` or `FLAG_SIGNATURE_ETH_SIGN`), ERC-1271 contract-based 
checks, nested ‚Äúmulti-sig inside multi-sig,‚Äù and special ‚Äúsapient‚Äù 
signatures. Each sub-signature or branch piece is prefixed by one byte: the top 
nibble is the ‚Äúflag type‚Äù and the bottom nibble contains per-flag 
configuration bits (like weight, sizes, or additional bits for `v`).
    21  
    22  ---
    23  
    24  ## **2. Top-level Signature Format**
    25  
    26  When `recover` is first invoked, it reads the **first byte** of the 
signature as `signatureFlag`. That byte is bit-packed as follows (with bit `0` 
as the least-significant bit):
    27  
    28  ```
    29   ‚îå‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ Bit 7 (0x80) : Static 
signature
    30   ‚îÇ ‚îå‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ Bit 6 (0x40) : 
Checkpointer usage flag
    31   ‚îÇ ‚îÇ ‚îå‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ Bit 5 (0x20) : Threshold 
size indicator (0 => 1 byte, 1 => 2 bytes)
    32   ‚îÇ ‚îÇ ‚îÇ  ‚îå‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ Bits 4..2 (0x1C) : Checkpoint 
size (encoded as an integer 0..7)
    33   ‚îÇ ‚îÇ ‚îÇ  ‚îÇ  ‚îå‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ Bit 1 (0x02) : "no chain id" 
signature type
    34   ‚îÇ ‚îÇ ‚îÇ  ‚îÇ  ‚îÇ ‚îå‚îÄ‚îÄ‚îÄ Bit 0 (0x01) : "chained" signature 
type
    35  [7 6 5 432 1 0]
    36  ```
    37  
    38  We can break this down more concretely:
    39  
    40  1. **Bit 7** set (`0x80`) indicates a static signature:
    41     - When set, the signature has been pre-stored in contract storage and
bypasses normal validation
    42     - Validation only checks:
    43       - That the stored expiry timestamp has not passed
    44       - That the stored signer matches the transaction sender (or is 
unset with `address(0)`)
    45  1. **Bit 6** set (`0x40`) means the signature includes an external 
**imageHash checkpointer**:
    46     - If set, the signature will contain:
    47       - The checkpointer contract `address`
    48       - A 3-byte length for the ‚Äúcheckpointer data‚Äù
    49       - That data, passed to 
`ICheckpointer(checkpointer).snapshotFor(...)`
    50  1. **Bits 4..2** (the field `((signatureFlag & 0x1c) >> 2)`) define the 
**checkpoint size** in bytes. Possible values are `0..7`. If this value is `N`, 
then the next `N` bytes of the signature after reading the flag (and optional 
checkpointer data) represent the **checkpoint**.
    51  1. **Bit 5** (`0x20`) sets how many bytes are used to read the 
threshold. If it is `0`, the threshold is read as 1 byte; if it is `1`, the 
threshold is read as 2 bytes. (Hence `( (signatureFlag & 0x20) >> 5 ) + 1`.)
    52  1. **Bit 1** (`0x02`) indicates the ‚Äúno chain id‚Äù signature. If set,
`_payload.noChainId` is true. This affects how `_payload.hash()` is computed.
    53  1. **Bit 0** (`0x01`) indicates the signature is **chained**. In that 
case, the code calls `recoverChained`, which processes multiple sub-signatures 
in sequence.
    54  
    55  Putting it together:
    56  
    57  - If bit `0` is set, we do a **chained** approach: The signature is 
composed of chunks, each chunk specifying a length and then a nested signature.
    58  - Otherwise, we do a ‚Äúregular‚Äù top-level parse: we read the 
checkpoint size, threshold size, then parse the ‚Äúbranch‚Äù for signers.
    59  
    60  ### **Example of a Top-level Signature Byte**
    61  
    62  Suppose the top-level `signatureFlag` is `0x74` in hex. Converting 
`0x74` to binary:
    63  
    64  ```
    65  0x74 = 01110100 in binary
    66          ^ ^ ^ ^
    67  bit 7:  0 (reserved)
    68  bit 6:  1 => checkpointer usage
    69  bit 5:  1 => threshold uses 2 bytes
    70  bits 4..2: 101 => checkpoint size = 5 bytes
    71  bit 1:  0 => normal (not "no chain id")
    72  bit 0:  0 => not chained
    73  ```
    74  
    75  From this:
    76  
    77  - We first read an `address` for the checkpointer, then read 3 bytes for
the checkpointer data length, etc.
    78  - We know we must parse **5 bytes** for the checkpoint value.
    79  - Then parse **2 bytes** for the threshold.
    80  - Then parse the remainder as the merkle-branch structure for signers.
    81  
    82  ---
    83  
    84  ## **3. Chained Signatures**
    85  
    86  When **bit 0** is set (the least-significant bit), the signature is 
**chained**. Instead of the usual approach (parsing threshold, checkpoint, etc. 
from that same byte), the code calls:
    87  
    88  ```solidity
    89  recoverChained(_payload, snapshot, _signature);
    90  ```
    91  
    92  A chained signature is a series of **signature chunks**, each chunk 
defined like this:
    93  
    94  ```
    95  [3-byte length] [chunk of that length]
    96  [3-byte length] [chunk of that length]
    97  ...
    98  ```
    99  
   100  Each chunk can itself be a top-level signature in the sense that it 
calls `recover(...)` again‚Äîexcept it ignores checkpointer details after the 
first chunk. The code enforces:
   101  
   102  - Each chunk recovers `(threshold, weight, imageHash, checkpoint)`.
   103  - If `weight < threshold`, it reverts with `LowWeightChainedSignature`.
   104  - The `checkpoint` must be **strictly less** than the previous chunk‚Äôs
`checkpoint`, ensuring correct ordering (`WrongChainedCheckpointOrder`).
   105  - All but the first chunk are interpreted as a ‚Äúconfiguration 
update‚Äù with a special ‚ÄúlinkedPayload.‚Äù
   106  
   107  This allows multiple signature instructions to be ‚Äúchained‚Äù in a 
single byte array.
   108  
   109  ```
   110     0           1           2           3
   111     |----- byte indices: 0..2 => 3-byte length L1
   112     |----- next L1 bytes => chunk #1
   113              ...
   114     |----- next 3 bytes => length L2
   115     |----- next L2 bytes => chunk #2
   116              ...
   117     |----- next 3 bytes => length L3
   118     |----- next L3 bytes => chunk #3
   119              ...
   120     <end of signature>
   121  ```
   122  
   123  Each chunk is itself a ‚Äútop-level style signature‚Äù minus the 
repeated checkpointer usage. The final `(threshold, weight, imageHash, 
checkpoint)` from the last chunk can be used to validate the overall signature.
   124  
   125  ---
   126  
   127  ## **4. Branch-Level Parsing**
   128  
   129  Regardless of whether it is a chained signature or a direct one, 
eventually the code calls:
   130  
   131  ```solidity
   132  recoverBranch(_payload, opHash, _signature)
   133  ```
   134  
   135  This function loops over the remainder of the signature, reading one 
byte at a time as the ‚Äúheader‚Äù for a sub-signature or branch item. We‚Äôll 
call that one byte `firstByte`. The code extracts:
   136  
   137  - `flag = (firstByte & 0xf0) >> 4;` (the top nibble)
   138  - The lower nibble is used as ‚Äúfree bits.‚Äù
   139  
   140  ### **4.1 Flag Values**

   275  ```
   276  
   277  #### Chained signatures
   278  
   279  If chained signatures are used, then the encoding becomes slightly more 
complex. The reason for this is that the **only** checkpointer that is used is 
the one that is defined by the wallet contract, **checkpointers provided by the 
state channel are ignored**.
   280  
   281  This is intended behavior, as the checkpointer is in charge of 
overseeing the state channel, thus it wouldn't be safe for the state channel to 
be able to provide a checkpointer, as it could self-authorize the state channel.
This would negate the purpose of having a checkpointer in the first place.
   282  
   283  There is a challenge with this, when a chained signature is encoded, the
configuration that corresponds to the wallet contract is provided **at the end 
of the chain**, which is a problem, because we need to obtain the snapshot at 
the beginning of the chain, in order to validate if the state channel ever "goes
over" the snapshot provided by the checkpointer.
   284  
   285  Additionally, intermediary configurations need to have their 
checkpointers provided, as it is needed to reconstruct the merkle root. However 
no checkpointer data is needed, as these intermediate checkpointers are not 
called.
   286  
   287  To solve this, when a chained signature is used, and the onchain 
configuration contains a checkpointer, the checkpointer of the **last** 
configuration is moved at the beginning of the chain, alongside its checkpointer
data, and no checkpointer data is provided for the intermediate signature parts.
   288  
   289  ```
   290       ┌─▶ 2nd. bit signals checkpointer    ┌──▶ The checkpointer and data
is only
   291       │                                    │    provided once, as part of
the
   292       │      ┌▶ 8th. bit signals chained   │    chained signatures, and 
not of
   293       ┴      ┴                             │    the individual parts
   294      0100 0001                             │
   295      ▲ 
┌───────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────┐
   296      │ │                                       3 byte Size               
│
   297    ──┴─│                                         ──────                  
│
   298    0x41│6974206973207265616c6C792073756E6E791aDd 001271 
6b656570206275696c64696e67...│─┐
   299        │──────────────────────────────────────── 
────────────────────────────────────│ │
   300        │Checkpointer address □                   Checkpointer data       
│ │
   301        
└─────────────────────╫───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ 
│
   302  ╔═══════════════════════════╝                                           
│
   303  ║    
┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
─┘
   304  ║    │
   305  ║    │     Payload signature                          ┌─▶ No 
checkpointer data
   306  ║    │    
┌───────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┐
